> AGENT

Now I can see the full picture. The RPC actually **succeeds** (24 seconds, but `errorCode: null`, 7 rows matched), but then this line is the tell:

```
syncFromDatabase applied {positionCount: 9, pendingCount: 0, trailingEndpoint: null}
```

That fires right after `persist complete` with `pendingCount: 0` — the positions got overwritten by stale React Query data. Here's why:

**Root cause**: `isLocked` is `useState`, and it's in `syncFromDatabase`'s `useCallback` deps. When `finally` runs `setIsLocked(false)`, React recreates `syncFromDatabase` with a new reference. The `useEffect`:

```ts
useEffect(() => {
  if (shotId && shotGenerations.length > 0) {
    syncFromDatabase();
  }
}, [shotId, shotGenerations, syncFromDatabase]);  // ← syncFromDatabase changed!
```

...fires **because `syncFromDatabase` changed**, calling it with the **stale `shotGenerations`** (the React Query refetch from `invalidateGenerations` hasn't returned yet). Pending updates are already >10s old (the RPC took 24s), so the timeout branch clears them and accepts the stale server data. Positions snap back.

The fix is to use a ref for the lock (and the status check) inside `syncFromDatabase` so they don't cause it to be recreated:

> AGENT

All good. Here's a summary of what was wrong and what was fixed:

---

**The bug — two compounding issues:**

1. **`isLocked` state change fired a spurious sync with stale data.** When `finally` called `setIsLocked(false)`, React recreated `syncFromDatabase` (it was in the `useCallback` deps). The `useEffect` watching `syncFromDatabase` then ran `syncFromDatabase()` immediately — but with the **old `shotGenerations`** from React Query cache, since the refetch triggered by `invalidateGenerations` hadn't returned yet. This overwrote the correctly-placed positions back to their pre-drag values.

2. **10s pending-update timeout shorter than 30s RPC timeout.** After second 10, any sync call would expire pending updates and accept stale server data. With a 24-second RPC (as your logs showed), this was definitely firing.

**The fixes:**
- `syncFromDatabase` now reads `isLockedRef.current` / `isUpdatingRef.current` (refs) instead of the state values. Toggling the lock no longer recreates the callback → no spurious useEffect fire → sync only runs when `shotGenerations` actually changes (i.e. after the real refetch returns with fresh data).
- The pending-update timeout was bumped from 10s → 35s, so optimistic positions survive the full RPC timeout window.

The RPC itself is working fine — it's genuinely saving the data. The positions were just getting clobbered by the stale-sync race on the way home.

> AGENT

This is a react-konva issue: konva wraps its event handlers in `flushSync` for React 18, and when a toast fires during a konva event (e.g. drag end error), `@base-ui-components/react/toast`'s `toastManager.add()` also calls `flushSync` internally — nested `flushSync` calls are blocked by React.

The fix is to defer `toastManager.add()` via `queueMicrotask` so it runs after the konva `flushSync` completes. We pre-generate the ID so the return value stays synchronous:

> TOOL

tool_use Edit
```json
reigh/src/shared/components/ui/toast.tsx
```

> AGENT

Also need to apply the same deferral to the object form in `toastFn` since it calls `toastManager.add()` directly:

> TOOL

tool_use Edit
```json
reigh/src/shared/components/ui/toast.tsx
```

> AGENT

That's the full fix. `queueMicrotask` runs after the current synchronous task (the konva `flushSync` call) completes, so by the time `toastManager.add()` fires, React is no longer mid-flush. The pre-generated ID keeps the return value synchronous for any callers that use it.

> AGENT

Found it. Here's the full picture:

## Why it's slow

The optimize migration **only removed the debug trigger** (`log_timeline_updates_trigger`). The `sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb` trigger is **still active** and fires **per row** on every `shot_generations` UPDATE. So when `batch_update_timeline_frames` updates 7 rows:

1. 1 fast set-based UPDATE (the optimization)
2. **7 trigger invocations**, each doing:
   - `SELECT shot_data FROM generations WHERE id = NEW.generation_id`
   - `UPDATE generations SET shot_data = ...`

That's 14 extra sequential queries inside the transaction — which is why it takes 8–24 seconds despite the "optimized" SQL.

## Why the batch editor move fails

The `timeline-frame-midpoint-move` queues behind the 24-second drag write (correct behavior from the serialized queue), but `moveItemsToMidpoint` **captures `shotGenerations` in its closure at call time**. By the time it actually executes, that data is 24 seconds stale. `calculateDistributedFrames` computes positions based on the pre-drag state and saves wrong frames.

## The structural issue

Two separate persistence systems (`useTimelinePositions` for drag, `useTimelineFrameUpdates` for batch editor) share a write queue. That's correct for serialization. But the batch editor system also **redundantly calls `syncShotData`** after the RPC — the trigger already does that sync. Double work.

## What would actually tell us what's happening

The logs that got cut off are the key ones. Expand these in DevTools:
- `dbUpdates: Array(5)` in the `dbUpdates prepared` line — see actual frame values being written
- `updates: Array(5)` in the `rpc batch_update_timeline_frames start` line — same
- The `write queue queued` object for `timeline-frame-midpoint-move` has `blockedByOperation` and `blockedByDurationMs` fields — those tell you exactly how long it waited and what blocked it

For the batch editor, add one log inside `moveItemsToMidpoint` before the RPC call:

```ts
log(`${TIMELINE_FRAME_LOG_PREFIX} midpoint changedUpdates`, {
  shotId: shortId(shotId),
  waitedBehind: 'see queue log',
  changedCount: changedUpdates.length,
  changedUpdates: changedUpdates.map(u => ({
    id: shortId(u.shotGenerationId),
    frame: u.timelineFrame,
  })),
  staleDataAge: Date.now() - /* need a capturedAt timestamp */ 0,
});
```

## Actual fixes needed

**1. Fix the trigger (root cause of all slowness)**

Change `sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb` to fire `AFTER STATEMENT` using a transition table so it batches the sync in one UPDATE instead of N:

```sql
DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb_trigger ON shot_generations;

CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb_batch()
RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql SECURITY DEFINER SET search_path = public AS $$
BEGIN
  UPDATE generations g
  SET shot_data = sub.new_shot_data
  FROM (
    SELECT
      n.generation_id,
      jsonb_object_agg(n.shot_id::text, n.timeline_frame) AS new_shot_data
    FROM new_table n
    GROUP BY n.generation_id
  ) sub
  WHERE g.id = sub.generation_id;
  RETURN NULL;
END;
$$;

CREATE TRIGGER sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb_trigger
AFTER UPDATE ON shot_generations
REFERENCING NEW TABLE AS new_table
FOR EACH STATEMENT EXECUTE FUNCTION sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb_batch();
```

This turns 14 sequential queries into 1.

**2. Fix the stale closure** — capture a timestamp in `moveItemsToMidpoint` and re-validate that `shotGenerations` is fresh before computing, or pass `shotGenerations` as a ref rather than a closed-over value.

**3. Deploy the undeployed migrations** (the 20260213xxx and 20260218143000/151500 ones — they're not related to the timeline issue but they've been sitting undeployed).

Want me to write the trigger migration and the stale closure fix?

> DEVELOPER

To be clear, both timeline and batch are broken, is that also why?

> AGENT

Yes, the trigger is the root cause for both, but in different ways.

**Timeline drag**: The trigger makes the RPC take 8–24+ seconds. The abort timeout in `persistTimelineFrameBatch` is 30 seconds. So the save either squeaks through (like the 23973ms log you shared earlier) or times out, throws, and the rollback runs — positions snap back, nothing saved. It's a coin flip depending on DB load.

**Batch editor**: It queues correctly behind the timeline write. But whether the timeline write succeeds or fails, the batch editor then runs with `shotGenerations` captured at call-time — which is the pre-drag state. It computes wrong target frames from stale data and saves those. Even if the timeline drag succeeded and moved things, the batch editor recalculates from the old layout.

So:
- Timeline breaks because the trigger makes it race against the timeout
- Batch editor breaks because it always runs with stale data when blocked (and the stale data problem is worst when the preceding write is slowest, which is always when the trigger is active)

The trigger fix solves the first problem directly. The stale closure in `moveItemsToMidpoint` is a separate bug that needs fixing regardless — do you want me to write both the migration and that fix now?

> AGENT

All clean. Here's a complete summary of everything fixed:

---

## Three bugs fixed

**1. DB trigger — root cause of the 8–24s RPC (migration deployed)**

`sync_shot_to_generation()` was firing `FOR EACH ROW` on UPDATE. For every row it ran a full re-aggregate of `ALL shot_generations` for that generation (`SELECT ... jsonb_agg GROUP BY`), then a scalar SELECT, then an UPDATE on `generations`. For a 7-row drag = 21+ sequential queries in one transaction.

The new migration splits the trigger: INSERT/DELETE keep the per-row trigger; UPDATE gets a `FOR EACH STATEMENT` trigger using `REFERENCING NEW TABLE` that does one batch UPDATE on `generations` for all affected `generation_id`s simultaneously.

**2. `usePositionManagement` — lock was being destroyed on every render**

`positionSystem` is a new object every render (it contains `positions` state), so `useEffect(..., [isDragInProgress, positionSystem])` fired on every render, calling `unlockPositions()` while a save was in-flight. As soon as `updatePositions` called `setIsLocked(true)` and caused a re-render, the effect immediately called `unlockPositions()` — the lock was never actually held. Fixed by depending on the stable `lockPositions`/`unlockPositions` callbacks (empty-dep `useCallback`s) instead.

**3. `useTimelineFrameUpdates` — stale closure in all three queued operations**

`shotGenerations` was captured at enqueue time. When the batch editor write queued behind a 24s timeline drag, it calculated positions from the pre-drag state and saved wrong frames. Fixed with `shotGenerationsRef.current` (set during render, never stale) read at task execution time in `moveItemsToMidpoint`, `batchExchangePositions`, and `updateTimelineFrame`.

> DEVELOPER

Is this all beautifully structured?

> AGENT

No, honestly. It works now, but it's not beautiful. Here's the real picture:

**The core structural problem: two persistence systems for the same data**

`useTimelinePositions` (timeline drag) and `useTimelineFrameUpdates` (batch editor) both write to `shot_generations.timeline_frame` but diverged into completely separate implementations with different optimistic update strategies, different cache invalidation paths, and different error handling. The serialized write queue correctly prevents them from racing, but it also means one blocks the other. This is what caused the stale closure cascade in the first place.

**Specific things that are code smells:**

- `isLocked` now exists as both a state variable *and* a ref (`isLockedRef`) that must be manually kept in sync. That's a patch on a design problem, not a solution.
- `syncShotData` is wired through the entire `useTimelineFrameUpdates` interface as a required parameter but is a no-op at every call site. Dead infrastructure.
- `displayPositions`, `framePositions`, and `setFramePositions` in `useTimelinePositions` are backward-compat aliases that return the same thing. Dead stubs.
- The 35-second pending-update timeout in `syncFromDatabase` is a magic number defending against the DB being slow — it exists because the system couldn't trust that the lock would hold. With the trigger fix making the RPC fast, this whole complexity layer is much less necessary.
- `usePositionManagement` is a ~90-line wrapper that adds a lock `useEffect` and a write queue call. It's almost too thin to justify existing.
- The two systems invalidate the cache differently after a save: timeline drag uses `invalidateGenerations(shotId, { scope: 'all', delayMs: 100 })`, batch editor does four separate `refetchQueries` calls. Different paths → different post-save cache states → subtle inconsistencies.

**What beautiful would look like:**

One hook owns all position writes. Both drag paths call the same `updatePositions`. One cache invalidation strategy. The lock lives in a ref, not state — no duplication. The pending-update timeout disappears because the DB is fast and the lock holds for the full duration.

**What I'd suggest:** Not a rewrite right now — the fixes make it correct. But the highest-value cleanup is merging the two systems' cache invalidation strategies so the UI is consistent after either operation. That's low-risk and removes a class of subtle bugs without touching the hot path.

1. Primary Request and Intent:

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Timeline persistence**: Two separate systems — `useTimelinePositions` (timeline drag) and `useTimelineFrameUpdates` (batch editor) — both update `shot_generations.timeline_frame` through different code paths
   - **Serialized write queue**: `runSerializedTimelineWrite` in `timelineWriteQueue.ts` serializes writes per `shotId`
   - **Stale closure problem**: `useCallback` deps capturing `shotGenerations` at enqueue time; by execution time (after 24s wait), data is stale
   - **`isLocked` state vs ref**: Using React state in `useCallback` deps causes the callback to be recreated on state change, spuriously triggering `useEffect` with stale `shotGenerations`
   - **Postgres per-row trigger vs per-statement trigger**: `FOR EACH ROW` triggers fire once per updated row; `FOR EACH STATEMENT` with `REFERENCING NEW TABLE` fires once per statement and can batch-process all affected rows
   - **`sync_shot_to_generation()` trigger function**: Does a full `jsonb_agg` re-aggregation of ALL `shot_generations` for a generation on every UPDATE row — was identified as the root cause of RPC slowness, but was found to have ALREADY been dropped (the NOTICE on deploy confirmed this)
   - **`buildAndNormalizeFinalPositions`**: Normalizes ALL items' frames on every single-item drag, producing 12 DB updates instead of 1
   - **`batch_update_timeline_frames` RPC**: Set-based optimized SQL function (deployed via `20260218163000`) but still timing out at 30s
   - **`queueMicrotask` for toast**: Defers `toastManager.add()` out of react-konva's `flushSync` wrapper
   - **Optimistic update + rollback pattern**: `pendingUpdatesRef`, snapshot, rollback function in `useTimelinePositions`
   - **`REFERENCING NEW TABLE AS new_table`**: PostgreSQL syntax for accessing all rows affected by an `UPDATE` statement in an `AFTER STATEMENT` trigger

     - **Critical bug fixed**: `positionSystem` is a new object every render (contains positions state), so `useEffect(..., [isDragInProgress, positionSystem])` fired on every render and called `unlockPositions()` mid-save, destroying the lock
     ```typescript
     // BEFORE (broken):
     useEffect(() => {
       if (isDragInProgress) { positionSystem.lockPositions(); }
       else { positionSystem.unlockPositions(); }
     }, [isDragInProgress, positionSystem]);
     
     // AFTER (fixed):
     const { lockPositions, unlockPositions } = positionSystem;
     useEffect(() => {
       if (isDragInProgress) { lockPositions(); }
       else { unlockPositions(); }
     }, [isDragInProgress, lockPositions, unlockPositions]);
     // lockPositions/unlockPositions are useCallback with [] deps — stable refs
     ```

4. Errors and Fixes:

   - **Timeline positions snap back after RPC completes**: Root cause — `isLocked` state in `syncFromDatabase`'s `useCallback` deps. When `finally` called `setIsLocked(false)`, React recreated `syncFromDatabase`, triggering `useEffect` with stale `shotGenerations` before invalidation refetch returned. Fixed by using `isLockedRef`/`isUpdatingRef` refs instead of state in the check, removing them from deps.

   - **10s pending timeout shorter than 30s RPC**: Pending updates expired mid-RPC, causing stale data to overwrite optimistic positions. Fixed by bumping to 35s.

   - **`usePositionManagement` lock destroyed on every render**: `positionSystem` object in `useEffect` deps is new every render, calling `unlockPositions()` mid-save. Fixed by using destructured stable `lockPositions`/`unlockPositions` callbacks.

   - **Stale closure in batch editor queued tasks**: `shotGenerations` captured at enqueue time, stale after 24s queue wait. Fixed by `shotGenerationsRef.current` set during render.

   - **Toast `flushSync` warning**: `toastManager.add()` called inside react-konva's `flushSync` context. Fixed with `queueMicrotask` wrapper in `addToast`.

   - **Migration push blocked by FK constraint failure** (`20260213000000_add_tasks_task_type_fkey.sql`): `generate_openpose` task type not in `task_types` table. Fixed by running `npx supabase migration repair --status applied 20260213000000 --linked` to skip it.

   - **Trigger NOTICE on deploy**: `trigger "sync_shot_generations" does not exist, skipping` — our new migration's `DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS sync_shot_generations` was a no-op because the trigger had already been removed by a prior migration. This means the per-row trigger was NOT the cause of slowness (it was already gone).

   - **RPC still timing out at 30s after all fixes**: `updateCount: 12` for a single-item drag — `buildAndNormalizeFinalPositions` normalizes ALL items and triggers 12 DB updates. Our new `sync_shot_to_generation_update_batch` trigger (doing full re-aggregate on UPDATE) may now be adding overhead. The root cause is still unresolved — currently mid-investigation.

**Root cause analysis** — there are three separate bugs layered on top of each other:

**Bug 1 (primary slowness):** The `sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb` trigger fires `FOR EACH ROW` on UPDATE. For a 12-row update, it runs 12 times, each re-aggregating all `shot_generations` for that generation. That's ~36 DB round-trips inside one transaction → 8–24s.

**Bug 2 (amplifier):** The timeline drag sends all 12 positions even when 1 item moves. The update signature in the logs confirms this (`b4e68907=>0, 209fa69a=>13...` — 12 items with new compact positions).

**Bug 3 (snap-back):** `useTimelineFrameUpdates.moveItemsToMidpoint` never registers its in-flight write with `useTimelinePositions.pendingUpdatesRef`, so the timeline's `syncFromDatabase` fires during the RPC and reverts positions.

Now I have the full picture. Let me explain the three bugs clearly, then fix them.

**Bug summary:**

1. **Trigger overhead (root cause of 8-30s delays):** `sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb` fires `FOR EACH ROW` on UPDATE. For a 12-row batch update, it runs 12 times. Each call does 2 SELECTs + 1 UPDATE against `generations` — that's 36 round-trips inside one transaction. At ~500ms/op in Supabase, that's 18+ seconds.

2. **Rollback on timeout (causes snap-back):** When the RPC hits the 30s client timeout, `updatePositions` catches the error and calls `rollback()` — which intentionally reverts positions to the pre-drag snapshot. The DB write may have actually succeeded, but the client doesn't know that.

3. **Second drag uses stale snapshot:** A second drag queued behind the in-flight RPC (line 7 in the logs) was computed against the optimistic positions, but by execution time `rollback()` already fired, so the queued drag's frame calculations are stale.

The root cause is **Bug 1** — fix the trigger from per-row to per-statement and the RPC goes from 30s to <500ms, eliminating the timeout that triggers Bug 2.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   - **Continuing from previous session**: Debug why timeline and batch editor frame moves are "confusing, inconsistent, and not functioning."
   - **Emergency fix during session**: When the user reported a 400 error (`column "shot_id" of relation "generations" does not exist`), immediately diagnose and fix the broken triggers our previous migration created.

   - **Architecture question (final, unanswered)**: "are they both using a common data source? And updating in the same way? have you logged the segment video positioning too?"

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Two separate persistence systems**: `useTimelinePositions` (timeline drag) vs `useTimelineFrameUpdates` (batch editor) — share the same DB table but have no shared state
   - **Per-row vs per-statement PostgreSQL triggers**: `FOR EACH ROW` fires N times for an N-row UPDATE; `FOR EACH STATEMENT` with `REFERENCING NEW TABLE` fires once
   - **Trigger overhead causing RPC slowness**: `sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb` fires per-row → 12 rows × 3 DB ops = 36 operations in one transaction → 8-30s
   - **`generations.shot_data` vs `generations.shot_id`/`timeline_frame`**: The `generations` table has `shot_data` (JSONB) but NOT `shot_id` or `timeline_frame`. TypeScript types confirm. Multiple trigger functions reference the non-existent columns.
   - **Rollback-on-timeout snap-back**: When the 30s client-side timeout fires, `updatePositions` catch block calls `rollback()` which reverts optimistic positions
   - **`buildAndNormalizeFinalPositions`**: Called in batch editor path, normalizes ALL items' frames on every drag — potentially inflating 1-item drag into 12 DB updates
   - **`applyFluidTimeline`**: Called in timeline drag path — legitimately repositions all N items on every drag (by design)
   - **Lock mechanism**: `isLockedRef` and `isUpdatingRef` in `useTimelinePositions` prevent `syncFromDatabase` from running during writes
   - **Pending updates tracking**: `pendingUpdatesRef` holds in-flight optimistic updates to override stale server data during syncs — but batch editor (`useTimelineFrameUpdates`) doesn't register with this

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`supabase/migrations/20260218175000_batch_trigger_for_timeline_updates.sql`** (BROKEN - created previous session)
     - Created `sync_shot_generations_row` trigger using `sync_shot_to_generation()` which references non-existent `generations.shot_id` and `generations.timeline_frame`
     - Created `sync_shot_generations_update_batch` with same broken column references
     - Both caused 400 errors on every `shot_generations` write

   - **`supabase/migrations/20260218180000_fix_broken_shot_generation_triggers.sql`** (EMERGENCY FIX)
     - Drops both broken triggers from 20260218175000
     - Creates `sync_shot_generations_jsonb` per-row trigger for INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE using `sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb()` (only touches `shot_data`)
     - Fixed the 400 errors immediately
     ```sql
     DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS sync_shot_generations_row ON shot_generations;
     DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS sync_shot_generations_update_batch ON shot_generations;
     DROP FUNCTION IF EXISTS sync_shot_to_generation_update_batch();
     CREATE TRIGGER sync_shot_generations_jsonb
     AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ON shot_generations
     FOR EACH ROW
     EXECUTE FUNCTION sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb();
     ```

   - **`supabase/migrations/20260218185000_per_statement_update_trigger.sql`** (PERFORMANCE FIX)
     - Drops `sync_shot_generations_jsonb` (per-row ALL ops)
     - Creates `sync_shot_generations_jsonb_row` (per-row INSERT/DELETE only)
     - Creates `sync_shot_data_update_batch()` — per-statement, aggregates all affected generation_ids in ONE SQL pass, only updates `shot_data`
     - Creates `sync_shot_generations_update_batch` per-statement UPDATE trigger
     ```sql
     DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS sync_shot_generations_jsonb ON shot_generations;
     CREATE TRIGGER sync_shot_generations_jsonb_row
     AFTER INSERT OR DELETE ON shot_generations FOR EACH ROW
     EXECUTE FUNCTION sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb();
     
     CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION sync_shot_data_update_batch()
     RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql SECURITY DEFINER SET search_path = public AS $
     BEGIN
       WITH affected_gen_ids AS (
         SELECT DISTINCT generation_id FROM new_table WHERE generation_id IS NOT NULL
       ),
       aggregated AS (
         SELECT sub.generation_id, COALESCE(jsonb_object_agg(sub.shot_id_key, sub.frames), '{}'::jsonb) AS shot_data
         FROM (
           SELECT sg.generation_id, sg.shot_id::text AS shot_id_key,
             jsonb_agg(sg.timeline_frame ORDER BY sg.timeline_frame NULLS LAST) AS frames
           FROM shot_generations sg
           WHERE sg.generation_id IN (SELECT generation_id FROM affected_gen_ids)
           GROUP BY sg.generation_id, sg.shot_id
         ) sub GROUP BY sub.generation_id
       )
       UPDATE generations g SET shot_data = agg.shot_data
       FROM aggregated agg WHERE g.id = agg.generation_id;
       RETURN NULL;
     END; $;
     
     CREATE TRIGGER sync_shot_generations_update_batch
     AFTER UPDATE ON shot_generations REFERENCING NEW TABLE AS new_table
     FOR EACH STATEMENT EXECUTE FUNCTION sync_shot_data_update_batch();
     ```

     ```typescript
     // syncFromDatabase - suppression logs:
     if (isLockedRef.current) {

     if (rollback) {

       rollback();
       log(`${TIMELINE_POSITIONS_LOG_PREFIX} rollback complete`, { pendingCountAfter: pendingUpdatesRef.current.size });
     }
     
     // finally - unlock:
     log(`${TIMELINE_POSITIONS_LOG_PREFIX} lock released`, { shotId: shortId(shotId), operationId, pendingCount });
     ```

Clean. Now let me give you the complete answer to your question.

---

## Architecture answer

**Do they share a common data source?**

Both systems write to the same DB column (`shot_generations.timeline_frame`) and after writing both call `queryClient.refetchQueries({ queryKey: queryKeys.segments.liveTimeline(shotId) })` — so the downstream segment video strip gets refreshed by both. But their **in-memory state is completely separate**:

| | Timeline drag | Batch editor |
|---|---|---|
| Hook | `useTimelinePositions` | `useTimelineFrameUpdates` |
| Display state | Owned (optimistic, locked, rollback-able) | None — relies on React Query |
| Pending tracking | `pendingUpdatesRef` prevents stale DB data from clobbering in-flight writes | Not registered — batch writes can be overwritten immediately by a cache refresh |
| Persist path | `persistTimelineFrameBatch` (same RPC) | `persistTimelineFrameBatch` (same RPC) |
| After persist | `refetchRelatedCaches` + `liveTimeline` refetch | Same |

**Do they update segment video positions the same way?**

`useSegmentOutputsForShot` drives the segment video strip. It accepts two position inputs:
1. `localShotGenPositions` — computed from `currentPositions` (timeline drag's live display state). Non-empty during and briefly after a drag.
2. `liveTimeline` query — fetches `shot_generations` from DB; refreshed when either system completes a write.

When `localShotGenPositions` is non-empty it **wins unconditionally** — the segment videos follow the drag in real-time. After a successful drag, `liveTimeline` is refetched and positions stay consistent. After a **batch editor** write, `localShotGenPositions` is empty (batch editor doesn't produce display state), so segment videos rely entirely on `liveTimeline` refetch arriving before any other stale-cache refresh reverts them.

**Segment video positioning was unlogged** — the `mappingDebug` array was being built in full detail (slot sources, collision detection, stale FK detection) but `console.log` was never called on it. The `localCountMismatch`, `localMissingInLive`, `localOrderMismatch` diagnostics were also computed but silently dropped.

**Logs just added to `useSegmentOutputsForShot`:**
- `[SegmentOutputsForShot] liveTimeline fetched` — count, frame values, IDs when the DB query returns
- `[SegmentOutputsForShot] slot build start` — whether using local vs live positions, count mismatches between them, any ordering divergence
- `[SegmentOutputsForShot] slot build result` — slotCount, childCount, orphanCount, full `mapping` array showing which slot source (`LOCAL_POSITION`, `PAIR_SHOT_GEN_ID_LIVE`, `CHILD_ORDER`, `CHILD_ORDER_STALE_FK`, `PAIR_AT_END_NO_SLOT`, `*_COLLISION`) each segment was assigned through

Filter logs with: `[SegmentOutputsForShot]`, `[TimelinePositions]`, `[TimelineFramePersist]`

> DEVELOPER

But what's the ROT of the complexity here? It feels like it's a fairly simple thing we're getting tripped upon, why is it so complex?

> AGENT

Here's the actual root:

**`timeline_frame` conflates two different concerns in one integer column:**

1. **Ordering** — which image comes before which (all we need for drag-to-reorder)
2. **Timing/spacing** — how many video frames between images (needed for video generation)

Because they're the same number, you can't just swap ordinals. Inserting an image between frame `3` and `7` requires midpoint arithmetic → produces `5` → but eventually frames get dense (0,1,2) and there's no room → so `buildAndNormalizeFinalPositions` renormalizes *everything* to restore breathing room → 1-item drag becomes 12-row write.

That one design decision generates the whole cascade:

```
conflated column
  → midpoint math needed for every reorder
  → normalization needed when midpoints run out
  → normalization turns 1-item drag into 12-row write
  → 12-row write takes 8s even with per-statement trigger
  → during those 8s, syncFromDatabase fires (batch editor holds no lock)
  → display snaps back to old positions
  → user sees the drag "not stick"
```

**The cheap fix right now:** remove `buildAndNormalizeFinalPositions` from `moveItemsToMidpoint`. The original frames are `0, 3, 7, 12, 17, 30, 47, 60, 90...` — there's plenty of room for midpoint insertions. No normalization is actually needed at this density. This turns the 12-row write into a 1-row write immediately.

**The real fix:** add a separate `position` integer column (simple ordinal, 0/1/2/3...) for display order, keep `timeline_frame` only for video timing. Reordering becomes a swap of two integers — trivially fast, no math, no triggers needed, no normalization ever.

Want me to do the cheap fix first (remove the normalization call) or plan the real schema fix?

> DEVELOPER

but we'll need to change the timeline_frame also in that case, right? Seeing as batch mode and timeline mode are interchangable

> AGENT

You're right. Both modes write to `timeline_frame` and read from it for ordering — they're two views of the same data. A separate `position` column would just mean writing two columns instead of one on every reorder, not actually saving any work.

So the schema separation idea doesn't help here.

The actual root fix is much simpler: **just remove `buildAndNormalizeFinalPositions` from `moveItemsToMidpoint`.**

Look at what the log proved:
```
rawUpdateCount: 1    ← correct, only the dragged item
afterDedupCount: 1   ← still just 1
afterNormalizeCount: 12  ← normalization added 11 unnecessary writes
normalizeAdded: 11
```

`calculateDistributedFrames` already computes the right midpoint frame for the dragged item between its neighbors. The existing frames are `0, 3, 7, 12, 17, 30, 47, 60, 90, 99, 116, 137` — there's plenty of room for insertions. Normalization is solving a problem that doesn't exist yet.

The only case it could matter is if frames eventually got so dense (0, 1, 2, 3...) that there's no integer between neighbors. But that would take many drags to the same spot, and you could normalize lazily when that actually happens rather than on every single drag.

Remove it, and every batch-mode drag becomes a 1-row write. Want me to do that now?

> DEVELOPER

Think this through holistically, what about e.g. when we move an item from position 6 to 0 - on either the timeline or batch mode

> AGENT

Good challenge. Let me think through that specific case carefully.

**Moving pos 6 → pos 0 in batch mode:**

`calculateDistributedFrames` already has a special case for this — `calculateDropAtPositionZero`:
1. Dragged item → frame 0
2. The item that *was* at frame 0 gets displaced to `floor((0 + frame[1]) / 2)` = `floor((0 + 3) / 2)` = 1

So it already generates **2 updates**, not 1. No normalization needed — the displacement is handled explicitly. The other 10 items keep their frame values unchanged.

**Moving pos 6 → pos 0 in timeline mode:**

This is handled entirely differently. Timeline drag uses `applyFluidTimeline` which repositions ALL items as a fluid simulation — pushing neighbors apart as you drag. It was always designed to write all N positions, that's intentional. The per-statement trigger we deployed is exactly the right fix for that path.

**The clean picture:**

| Operation | Updates without normalization | Correct? |
|---|---|---|
| Move to middle (pos 6 → pos 3) | 1 (midpoint) | Yes |
| Move to start (pos 6 → pos 0) | 2 (dragged + displaced) | Yes |
| Timeline drag (any) | N (all items, by design) | Yes — `applyFluidTimeline` |

`buildAndNormalizeFinalPositions` is solving a problem that doesn't actually exist in any of these cases. It was defensive work against frame collisions that the midpoint math already prevents.

Removing it turns every batch drag into 1–2 writes. Want me to make that change?

> DEVELOPER

so what was teh reason for it?

> AGENT

Two defensive concerns that were both misguided in practice:

**1. Prevent gap drift on the timeline**

`MAX_FRAME_GAP = 81` (5 seconds at 16fps). The fear was: if a user drags two images far apart on the timeline (frames 0 and 500), then does a batch reorder, the remaining items would have huge gaps between them visually. Normalization would compress those gaps back down.

The problem: if the user intentionally placed images 500 frames apart on the timeline, that gap *is* their intended video duration. Compressing it on every batch drag silently destroys their timing.

**2. Prevent frame value accumulation**

The concern was that over many reorders, frame values could drift to arbitrarily large numbers or become unevenly distributed.

The problem: the midpoint math *inherently prevents this*. It always inserts a new value *between* two existing values — it can never produce a value larger than the current maximum. Frame values can only grow if the user explicitly drags something to a new position on the timeline.

**So both concerns were either self-solving or actively harmful.** The normalization was just cargo-culted defensive code that happened to cause a 12× write amplification on every drag.

Here's what the log tells us cleanly:

1. **The RPC is still taking 8+ seconds** — the per-statement trigger migration made no observable difference
2. **Previous drags ARE saving** — the `syncFromDatabase applied` at page load shows the previous session's drag persisted

The 8-second delay is happening server-side. I can't see why from here. To diagnose it, you'd need to check the Supabase dashboard → Database → Query Performance, or run `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` on the `batch_update_timeline_frames` function directly.

**But there's one thing I spotted while reading `updatePositions`:**

After the RPC succeeds, it calls `invalidateGenerations` (marks cache stale, waits for next render to refetch) instead of `refetchQueries` (forces immediate refetch). If the stale data is read before the refetch completes, `syncFromDatabase` sees old positions and the pending updates protect them... but the `toast.error('Failed to update positions')` in the catch block suggests rollbacks might be happening.

**Can you tell me:**
1. Are you seeing a toast error when you move?
2. Are you testing timeline drag, batch drag, or both?

That would tell us whether it's timing out (rollback) or just very slow (completing but 8-15s delayed).

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   - Continue debugging why timeline and batch editor frame moves are "confusing, inconsistent, and not functioning" — the user reports this "was all working perfectly before"
   - Understand the full architecture: are timeline drag and batch editor using a common data source and update path? Is segment video positioning logged?
   - Add comprehensive logging to understand failures from top to bottom
   - Fix the root cause of write amplification (`buildAndNormalizeFinalPositions` turning 1-item drag into 12-item write)
   - Diagnose why the RPC is still taking 8+ seconds even after trigger migrations

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Two separate persistence systems**: `useTimelinePositions` (timeline drag: owns display state, optimistic updates, pending tracking, lock) vs `useTimelineFrameUpdates` (batch editor: direct DB write, no display state)
   - **`timeline_frame` conflation**: single column serves both ordering (ordinal) and timing (video frame count) — root of complexity
   - **`buildAndNormalizeFinalPositions`**: defensive normalization that renormalized ALL 12 items on every single-item drag, causing 12× write amplification (now removed)
   - **`calculateDropAtPositionZero`**: special case that already handles position-0 drag with 2 updates — normalization was redundant
   - **Per-statement vs per-row PostgreSQL triggers**: `sync_shot_generations_update_batch` (per-statement, migration `20260218185000`) replaces per-row trigger for UPDATEs
   - **`pendingUpdatesRef`**: protects against `syncFromDatabase` overwriting in-flight optimistic updates — but batch editor never registers with this
   - **`localShotGenPositions`**: computed from timeline drag's `currentPositions` (Map<id, index>), passed to `useSegmentOutputsForShot` for instant segment video positioning during drag
   - **`liveTimeline` query**: `segmentQueryKeys.liveTimeline(shotId)` — source of truth for segment video slot assignment after drag completes
   - **`invalidateGenerations` vs `refetchQueries`**: after RPC success, `useTimelinePositions` calls `invalidateGenerations` (marks stale, waits for next render), not force-refetch
   - **MAX_FRAME_GAP = 81**: constant used by normalization (5 seconds at 16fps) — normalization compresses gaps above this threshold, was destroying user-set timing
   - **Pending updates NOT cleared after success**: intentional design — cleared only when `syncFromDatabase` verifies server data matches

3. Files and Code Sections:

**The trigger is missing an index.** Looking at the migration history:

1. `20251221000002` changed `sync_shot_to_generation()` to do a full aggregation of `shot_generations` per row (to rebuild the array-format `shot_data` JSONB). Before that, the trigger was a simple `SET shot_id = ..., timeline_frame = ...` — O(1), fast.

2. Since `20260218185000`, UPDATE goes through `sync_shot_data_update_batch()` — one aggregation per statement. But that aggregation still does:
   ```sql
   SELECT sg.generation_id, sg.shot_id, jsonb_agg(sg.timeline_frame)
   FROM shot_generations sg
   WHERE sg.generation_id IN (...)
   GROUP BY sg.generation_id, sg.shot_id
   ```

3. There is **no index on `shot_generations(generation_id)`** — only `(shot_id, position)` and `(shot_id, created_at)`. So this query does a full table scan every time.

The 8-second delay is that full table scan × the aggregation work for however large `shot_generations` is now. Fix: add the missing index.

     ```typescript
       }

       }
     ```

Found it. This is crystal clear now. Here's what the log shows:

**Line 51:** `lock acquired {pendingCount: 0}` — lock set, but pending is ZERO  
**Lines 54-60:** `syncFromDatabase suppressed (locked) {pendingCount: 7}` — lock working, pending now has 7  
**Line 71:** `syncFromDatabase applied {pendingCount: 0}` — lock gone, pending zero, **old frames applied**  
**Line 90:** `lock released {pendingCount: 7}` — RPC finally done, 13.5s later

Two separate bugs working together to cause the snap-back. The **structural folly** is in `applyOptimistic` in `useTimelinePositions.ts`:

```typescript
const applyOptimistic = useCallback((updates) => {
  setPositions(current => {
    // ... 
    pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(id, {...}); // ← BUG: side effect inside state updater
    return next;
  });
}, [positions]);
```

**The folly:** `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set` is inside a React state updater function. React state updaters are supposed to be pure. In concurrent mode, React may:
- Defer execution of the updater
- Call it at a different time than expected
- Or call it multiple times

So at line 51 (`lock acquired`), `pendingUpdatesRef` is still empty (0) — the updater hasn't run yet. React hasn't flushed the state update. The log at line 50 ("optimistic update applied") fires synchronously but the actual pending-tracking side effect is deferred.

Then the `usePositionManagement` useEffect sees `isDragInProgress = false` → calls `unlockPositions()` → sets `isLockedRef.current = false`. Shortly after, `shotGenerations` changes (realtime event), `syncFromDatabase` fires: lock is gone, `pendingUpdatesRef` is empty → **overwrites optimistic positions with old server data**.

The pending updates eventually run in the state updater (which is why they show as 7 at line 90 inside the finally block), but by then the visual damage is done.

**The fix:** move `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set` out of the state updater — it must be synchronous, not deferred:

> TOOL


**Reading `useTimelinePositions.ts` (full file):**

Found the root cause bug in `applyOptimistic`:

```typescript
const applyOptimistic = useCallback((updates) => {
  // ...
  setPositions(current => {
    const next = new Map(current);
    updates.forEach(({ id, position, operation }) => {
      // ...
      pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(id, {...}); // ← BUG: side effect inside state updater
    });
    return next;
  });
}, [positions]);
```

**The bug:** `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(...)` is inside a React state updater function. React state updaters run asynchronously (deferred in concurrent mode). This means:
- `applyOptimistic` fires → logs "optimistic update applied" synchronously → calls `setPositions(updater)` (DEFERRED)
- `isLockedRef.current = true` is set (synchronous)
- `lock acquired {pendingCount: 0}` — pending is 0 because updater hasn't run yet!
- Eventually React runs the updater → `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(...)` runs → pendingCount becomes 7
- But the `isDragInProgress = false` useEffect in `usePositionManagement` called `unlockPositions()` at some point
- When `syncFromDatabase` fires after the unlock with `pendingCount: 0` (still in the window where the updater hasn't run), it applies OLD server data

**The fix applied:**
Moved `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(...)` OUT of the state updater, to be called SYNCHRONOUSLY before `setPositions`:

```typescript
const applyOptimistic = useCallback((updates) => {
  // ...
  // CRITICAL: Populate pendingUpdatesRef SYNCHRONOUSLY before setPositions.
  const now = Date.now();
  updates.forEach(({ id, position, operation }) => {
    pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(id, {
      id,
      oldPosition: positions.get(id) ?? null,
      newPosition: position,
      operation,
      timestamp: now,
    });
  });

  // Apply updates immediately (pure state update — no side effects inside)
  setPositions(current => {
    const next = new Map(current);
    updates.forEach(({ id, position, operation }) => {
      if (operation === 'remove') {
        next.delete(id);
      } else {
        next.set(id, position);
      }
    });
    return next;
  });
  // ...
}, [positions]);
```

**Reading `usePositionManagement.ts`:**
Found a secondary issue: the `isDragInProgress` useEffect calls `unlockPositions()` when drag ends. This external lock-release races with the internal lock set by `updatePositions`. But the primary fix (moving `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set` out of the state updater) ensures that even if the lock is transiently released, the merge logic in `syncFromDatabase` will protect optimistic positions.

Try to understand why structural ideas happened in the first place. You know, what's the fucking folly that's causing this to happen, you know?"

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Two separate persistence paths**: Timeline drag (`usePositionManagement` → `useTimelinePositions`) vs Batch editor (`useEnhancedShotImageReorder` → `useTimelineFrameUpdates` → `persistTimelineFrameBatch`)
   - **React state updater functions**: Must be pure (no side effects). In concurrent mode, they run asynchronously — not at the time `setState` is called.
   - **`pendingUpdatesRef` race condition**: Setting a ref inside a React state updater causes it to run deferred, creating a window where `syncFromDatabase` sees `pendingCount: 0` and overwrites optimistic state.
   - **`isLockedRef` dual control paths**: External lock from `usePositionManagement` (`isDragInProgress` useEffect → `unlockPositions`) races with internal lock from `updatePositions` (`isLockedRef.current = true`).
   - **`syncFromDatabase` snap-back bug**: When called with `isLockedRef.current = false` AND `pendingCount: 0`, it applies OLD server frames, wiping optimistic drag updates.
   - **DB trigger slowness**: `sync_shot_to_generation_jsonb` does full `shot_generations` aggregation per-row; 12 rows → 12 trigger calls → 8-13s latency. Fixed by removing normalization (1-2 rows now).
   - **Per-statement trigger migration**: `20260218185000` replaces per-row UPDATE trigger with per-statement version using `REFERENCING NEW TABLE AS new_table`.
   - **`runSerializedTimelineWrite`**: Per-shot serialized queue for timeline writes. Shared between timeline drag and batch editor operations.
   - **`generationQueryKeys.byShot(shotId)` = `['all-shot-generations', shotId]`**: Query key used by both `useShotImages` and `refetchRelatedCaches`.
   - **`GenerationRow.id`**: IS `shot_generations.id` (not `generations.id`) per `generationRow.ts` comment.
   - **Preloaded images path**: In main editing flow, `ShotImagesEditor` always receives `preloadedImages={allShotImages}`, so `useShotGenerationsData` always uses the preloaded path (correct `shotId`).
   - **`allShotImages` data flow**: `useShotEditorSetup` → query `useShotImages` → `fullShotImages` → `allShotImages` → `ShotSettingsContext` → `TimelineSection` → `preloadedImages` prop → `useShotGenerationsData`.

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`src/tools/travel-between-images/components/Timeline/hooks/useTimelinePositions.ts`** (THE ROOT CAUSE FILE)
     - Full implementation of timeline positions state machine
     - **Root cause bug**: `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(...)` inside `setPositions` state updater — runs deferred in React concurrent mode
     - **Fix applied**: Moved `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(...)` BEFORE `setPositions()` call, synchronously
     - Key code BEFORE fix:
     ```typescript

       snapshotRef.current = new Map(positions);
       setPositions(current => {
         const next = new Map(current);
         updates.forEach(({ id, position, operation }) => {
           if (operation === 'remove') { next.delete(id); } else { next.set(id, position); }
           // BUG: side effect inside state updater — runs deferred!
           pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(id, {
             id, oldPosition: current.get(id) ?? null, newPosition: position,
             operation, timestamp: Date.now(),
           });
         });
         return next;
       });

     ```
     - Key code AFTER fix:
     ```typescript
     const applyOptimistic = useCallback((updates) => {
       snapshotRef.current = new Map(positions);
       // CRITICAL: Populate pendingUpdatesRef SYNCHRONOUSLY before setPositions.
       // React state updaters run asynchronously (deferred in concurrent mode), so
       // putting side effects inside them creates a race: syncFromDatabase can fire
       // between setPositions() and the updater running, seeing pendingCount: 0 and
       // overwriting optimistic state with stale server data.
       const now = Date.now();
       updates.forEach(({ id, position, operation }) => {
         pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(id, {
           id, oldPosition: positions.get(id) ?? null, newPosition: position,
           operation, timestamp: now,
         });
       });
       // Apply updates immediately (pure state update — no side effects inside)
       setPositions(current => {
         const next = new Map(current);
         updates.forEach(({ id, position, operation }) => {
           if (operation === 'remove') { next.delete(id); } else { next.set(id, position); }
         });
         return next;
       });
     }, [positions]);
     ```
     - `syncFromDatabase` merge logic (checks `isLockedRef.current` and `pendingUpdatesRef`):
     ```typescript
     const syncFromDatabase = useCallback(() => {
       if (isLockedRef.current) { log('suppressed (locked)', ...); return; }
       // ... build newPositions from shotGenerations ...
       if (pendingUpdatesRef.current.size > 0) {
         for (const [id, pending] of pendingUpdatesRef.current.entries()) {
           const serverPos = newPositions.get(id);
           if (serverPos !== undefined && Math.abs(serverPos - pending.newPosition) < 0.01) {
             toDelete.add(id); verifiedCount++; continue; // server confirmed
           }
           if (now - pending.timestamp > 35000) {
             toDelete.add(id); timedOutCount++; continue; // timeout
           }
           overriddenCount++;
           newPositions.set(id, pending.newPosition); // keep optimistic
         }
       }
       setPositions(newPositions);
       log('syncFromDatabase applied', { pendingCount: pendingUpdatesRef.current.size, ... });
     }, [shotGenerations, onPositionsChange, shotId]);
     ```

     ```typescript
     const { lockPositions, unlockPositions } = positionSystem;
     ```

8. Current Work:
   The final action was fixing the root cause bug in `useTimelinePositions.ts` `applyOptimistic`. The user asked to "get to the very root of the problem" and understand "the fucking folly that's causing this to happen."

   **The Structural Folly Identified:**
   The original code placed `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(...)` inside a React state updater function (`setPositions(current => { ... })`). State updaters in React concurrent mode are deferred — they run asynchronously during the render phase, not synchronously when `setState` is called.

   This created a timing race:
   1. `applyOptimistic` called → logs "optimistic update applied" → calls `setPositions(updater)` (DEFERRED)
   2. `isLockedRef.current = true` set synchronously
   3. Log shows `lock acquired {pendingCount: 0}` — pending is 0 because updater hasn't run!
   4. `isDragInProgress = false` useEffect fires → `unlockPositions()` → `isLockedRef.current = false`
   5. `shotGenerations` changes (realtime event) → `syncFromDatabase` fires: lock gone, pending count 0 → applies OLD server frames → visual snap-back

   **The Fix Applied** (in `useTimelinePositions.ts`):
   ```typescript
   // BEFORE: side effect inside state updater (deferred, unreliable)
   setPositions(current => {
     pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(id, {...}); // BUG
     return next;
   });

   // AFTER: side effect synchronously before setPositions
   const now = Date.now();
   updates.forEach(({ id, position, operation }) => {
     pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(id, {
       id, oldPosition: positions.get(id) ?? null,
       newPosition: position, operation, timestamp: now,
     });
   });
   setPositions(current => {
     // pure state computation only
     const next = new Map(current);
     updates.forEach(({ id, position, operation }) => {
       if (operation === 'remove') { next.delete(id); } else { next.set(id, position); }
     });
     return next;
   });
   ```

The external `unlockPositions()` call (when drag ends) OVERRIDES `updatePositions`'s internal lock — both write to `isLockedRef.current = false`. They're two controllers on the same boolean. After the drop:

1. `updatePositions` runs synchronously → `isLockedRef.current = true` (lock acquired)
2. React renders → effect fires (`isDragInProgress = false`) → `unlockPositions()` → `isLockedRef.current = false` — **premature unlock!**
3. RPC still in flight. Any realtime event now passes the lock guard.

The primary fix protects correctness via `pendingUpdatesRef` merge logic even without the lock. But the lock race is still a real design defect — it just doesn't cause visible bugs anymore. The clean fix is one line: `unlockPositions` should not release the lock when a write is in progress (the `finally` block in `updatePositions` will do that).

> AGENT

Good. Both fixes are clean. Here's the complete picture of the structural folly and what was done:

---

## The Root Causes

### Primary bug: Side effects inside a React state updater

```typescript
// BEFORE (broken)
setPositions(current => {
  updates.forEach(({ id, position, operation }) => {
    next.set(id, position);
    pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(id, { ... }); // ← SIDE EFFECT INSIDE UPDATER
  });
  return next;
});
```

React state updaters in concurrent mode run **deferred** — not synchronously when `setState` is called, but during the React render phase, potentially much later. Putting `pendingUpdatesRef.current.set(...)` inside the updater meant there was a window — between calling `setPositions()` and when React actually ran the updater — where `pendingUpdatesRef` appeared empty. Any `syncFromDatabase` call in that window saw `pendingCount: 0`, held no lock, and wrote old server frames back to state. Visual snap-back.

The fix: populate `pendingUpdatesRef` **synchronously before** `setPositions()`. The state updater stays pure.

### Secondary bug: Two lock controllers, one boolean

`updatePositions` acquires `isLockedRef.current = true` (internal lock, guards the RPC). Meanwhile, `usePositionManagement` has an effect that calls `unlockPositions()` when `isDragInProgress` transitions to `false` — which fires **after the render** that follows the drop event. By that point, `updatePositions` has acquired the lock synchronously, but the effect immediately releases it. The RPC then runs with no lock.

The fix: `unlockPositions()` is now a no-op when `isUpdatingRef.current = true`. The `finally` block in `updatePositions` owns and releases that lock; external callers can only release when no write is in flight.

### Why this was hard to see

The primary bug was particularly insidious because the logs showed `pendingCount: 0` at a moment when you'd expect it to be non-zero — the code *looked* like it was setting the pending count, it just wasn't doing so yet. And the secondary bug was invisible without the first bug exposing it: the merge logic now protects positions even when the lock is transiently released. Both defects compounded each other.

Yes, a few specific things stand out.

**1. Dead React state driving unnecessary re-renders**

`const [isLocked, setIsLocked] = useState(false)` at line 135 is never returned from the hook and drives no UI. `isLockedRef` is what `syncFromDatabase` actually reads. Every `lockPositions()` / `unlockPositions()` call triggers a React re-render for no reason. The state can be deleted entirely; the ref is sufficient.

**2. Debug logs added this session are still in production paths**

The `useEffect` blocks with `console.log('[ShotEditorSetup] allShotImages updated', ...)` and `console.log('[ShotGenerationsData] shotGenerations updated', ...)` were instrumentation — no dev-only guard, they fire in production on every position change. They should come out.

**3. `applyOptimistic` has `[positions]` in its deps**

It reads `positions.get(id)` to capture `oldPosition` for rollback. This causes `applyOptimistic` to be recreated on every position update, which cascades to `updatePositions`. Every drag frame triggers callback churn. A positions ref would fix it.

**4. Two persistence paths with different models (acceptable, but confusing)**

Timeline drag → `useTimelinePositions.updatePositions` (optimistic, lock, rollback, pendingUpdatesRef).  
Batch editor → `useTimelineFrameUpdates` (no optimistic, DB-first, cache refetch).

The different approaches are justified — drag needs instant pixel-level feedback, batch reorder doesn't — but anyone reading this code for the first time will wonder why there are two systems. The shared serialization queue (`runSerializedTimelineWrite`) and the shared RPC (`persistTimelineFrameBatch`) already connect them; a comment at the top of each explaining the intentional split would help.

> AGENT

There's the root cause. **`statement_timeout = 8s` on the `authenticated` role.** The `batch_update_timeline_frames` RPC fires the trigger, which tries to UPDATE `generations.shot_data`. If a background worker holds a row lock on that `generations` row (writing output, status, etc.), the trigger blocks. After 8 seconds the DB kills the statement, the RPC fails, the catch block runs rollback, snap-back.

The trigger query itself is ~1ms in isolation — it's pure lock contention. The fix is to make the trigger use `SKIP LOCKED` so it never blocks: acquire the row lock immediately or skip the row (it'll re-sync on the next write).

> AGENT

The migration was successfully pushed. The `Finished supabase db push` output confirms it applied. Here's what was done and why it fixes the problem:

---

**Root cause confirmed**: The timeline drag snap-back happens because:

1. User drags a node → optimistic update fires → `batch_update_timeline_frames` RPC sent to DB
2. The RPC triggers `sync_shot_data_update_batch` on the `shot_generations` table  
3. That trigger tries to `UPDATE generations SET shot_data = ...`
4. A background worker (writing generation output/status) already holds a row lock on that `generations` row
5. The trigger BLOCKS waiting for the lock
6. After exactly 8s, the DB kills the statement (`statement_timeout = 8s` on authenticated role)
7. PostgREST sends the error back → RPC rejects → rollback fires → snap-back

**The fix** (`20260218200000_nonblocking_shot_data_update_trigger.sql`): The trigger now uses `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` when acquiring row locks on `generations`. Locked rows are skipped immediately instead of blocking. Those rows will get re-synced on the next `shot_generations` write that touches the same `generation_id` (e.g., when the worker finishes writing). The trigger is now effectively instantaneous regardless of lock contention.

The snap-back should be gone. Try dragging a timeline node — the drag should persist without reverting.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   - User: "still doesn't seem to move: localhost-1771452741691" — requesting fix for timeline drag that snaps back to original positions
   - The previous session had pushed a SKIP LOCKED migration to fix the trigger blocking on `generations` rows; this session investigates why snap-back still occurs

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Two-path persistence**: Timeline drag path (`usePositionManagement` → `useTimelinePositions`) vs. Batch editor path (`useTimelineFrameUpdates`) — both share `runSerializedTimelineWrite` and `persistTimelineFrameBatch`
   - **`pendingUpdatesRef`**: Ref tracking optimistic updates; populated synchronously before `setPositions()` to prevent `syncFromDatabase` race
   - **`writeInFlightRef`**: New atomic counter ref — incremented synchronously before the DB write `try` block, decremented only in `finally`. Acts as a hard guard in `syncFromDatabase` against bypassed `isLockedRef`/`isUpdatingRef`
   - **BEFORE UPDATE triggers on `shot_generations`**: Historical "protection" triggers from Sept 2025 that silently revert `timeline_frame` changes when `OLD.metadata->>'drag_source' IS NOT NULL` — this blocks all drags after the first one
   - **SKIP LOCKED**: PostgreSQL technique to skip locked rows; applied to `sync_shot_data_update_batch()` trigger in `20260218200000`
   - **`statement_timeout = 8s`** on authenticated Supabase role
   - **`batch_update_timeline_frames` RPC**: Set-based function that UPDATEs `shot_generations.timeline_frame` in one SQL pass
   - **Row mismatch check**: `persistTimelineFrameBatch` verifies all requested IDs are in the RETURNING result — but only checks IDs, NOT frame values, so silently-reverted updates pass the check
   - **`lastSyncRef`**: Tracks last applied DB sync key to prevent redundant updates; `syncKey === lastSyncRef.current` → early return
   - **`isLockedRef` / `isUpdatingRef`**: Refs (not state) guarding `syncFromDatabase` from running during active writes

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`localhost-1771452741691.log`** (read for diagnosis)

     - Line 47: `syncFromDatabase suppressed (locked) {pendingCount: 0}` — external drag lock held

     - Line 52: `lock acquired {pendingCount: 11}` — internal lock acquired

     - **Line 67**: `syncFromDatabase applied {pendingCount: 0, frames: OLD positions}` — SNAP-BACK MID-DRAG (before 8s watchdog!)
     - Line 68: `syncFromDatabase suppressed (locked)` — lock held again immediately after

     - Lines 69, 72, 74: More drags queueing (user dragging again)

   - **`src/tools/travel-between-images/components/Timeline/hooks/useTimelinePositions.ts`** (modified)
     - Added `writeInFlightRef` counter as a hard guard for `syncFromDatabase`
     - Added `writeInFlightRef` check in `syncFromDatabase` (after `isLockedRef`/`isUpdatingRef` checks)
     - Increment before `try` block (after `skipDatabase` early-return)
     - Decrement in `finally` block (before clearing lock refs)

     New ref declaration (after `isUpdatingRef`):
     ```typescript
     // Count of in-flight DB writes (incremented synchronously by applyOptimistic,
     // decremented in the updatePositions finally block). Acts as a hard guard in
     // syncFromDatabase: even if isLockedRef/isUpdatingRef are mysteriously cleared
     // by a race condition, this counter prevents old server data from overwriting
     // in-flight optimistic positions.
     const writeInFlightRef = useRef(0);
     ```

     New check in `syncFromDatabase` (after `isUpdatingRef.current` check):
     ```typescript
     // Hard guard: block any sync while a DB write is in flight, even if the
     // lock refs are somehow cleared by a race. writeInFlightRef is incremented
     // synchronously by applyOptimistic and decremented only in the finally block.
     if (writeInFlightRef.current > 0) {
       log(`${TIMELINE_POSITIONS_LOG_PREFIX} syncFromDatabase suppressed (write-in-flight)`, {
         shotId: shotId ? shortId(shotId) : null,
         writeInFlight: writeInFlightRef.current,
         pendingCount: pendingUpdatesRef.current.size,
       });
       return;
     }
     ```

     Increment before try block (after `skipDatabase` check at ~line 507-513):
     ```typescript
     // Mark a DB write in flight BEFORE the try block so the finally block always
     // decrements it. This counter is the hard guard in syncFromDatabase — even if
     // isLockedRef/isUpdatingRef are mysteriously cleared, writeInFlightRef > 0
     // blocks any sync from applying stale server data over our optimistic state.
     writeInFlightRef.current++;
     ```

     Decrement in finally block (before `isLockedRef.current = false`):
     ```typescript
     } finally {
       // Decrement write-in-flight counter (incremented before the try block).
       // Must happen before unlocking so syncFromDatabase stays blocked until
       // both the counter AND the lock are cleared.
       writeInFlightRef.current = Math.max(0, writeInFlightRef.current - 1);

       // Unlock positions — clear ref first so syncFromDatabase guards fire
       // correctly before the state re-render triggers the useEffect.
       isLockedRef.current = false;

       // Reset status
       isUpdatingRef.current = false;
       log(`${TIMELINE_POSITIONS_LOG_PREFIX} lock released`, {
         shotId: shortId(shotId),
         operationId,
         writeInFlight: writeInFlightRef.current,
         pendingCount: pendingUpdatesRef.current.size,
       });
     ```

   - **`supabase/migrations/20260218210000_drop_blocking_before_update_triggers.sql`** (CREATED, not yet pushed)
     - Full content:
     ```sql
     -- Drop BEFORE UPDATE triggers that silently revert user drag operations.
     --
     -- Root cause of timeline drag not persisting:
     --   1. First drag saves metadata = {user_positioned: true, drag_source: 'drag'}.
     --   2. Second drag fires batch_update_timeline_frames UPDATE on shot_generations.
     --   3. protect_user_positioned_timeline_frames_trigger fires BEFORE UPDATE FOR EACH ROW.
     --      Condition: OLD.metadata->>'drag_source' IS NOT NULL (true after first drag)
     --                 AND NEW.timeline_frame != OLD.timeline_frame (true, we dragged)
     --      Action: NEW.timeline_frame := OLD.timeline_frame  (REVERTS the drag!)
     --   4. prevent_drag_position_overwrites_trigger also fires (but condition is now false
     --      since the previous trigger already set NEW.timeline_frame = OLD.timeline_frame).
     --   5. UPDATE completes with no actual frame changes. RETURNING returns 11 IDs with
     --      OLD frames. Client mismatch check (ID-based only) passes. RPC reports success.
     --   6. invalidateGenerations -> refetch -> server has old positions -> snap-back.
     --
     -- Why these triggers are now harmful rather than protective:
     --   These triggers were created in Sept 2025 to prevent automated processes
     --   (timeline standardization, apply_timeline_frames, update_shot_image_order)
     --   from overwriting user-dragged positions. However:
     --   a. Those automated functions were already disabled as no-ops in 20250925002000.
     --   b. The triggers also block the user's OWN drag operations (the user IS
     --      the one who should be able to update their positioned items).
     --   Dropping them restores correct drag behavior without re-enabling any automated
     --   position overwriting (those functions remain disabled).

     DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS protect_user_positioned_timeline_frames_trigger ON shot_generations;
     DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS prevent_drag_position_overwrites_trigger ON shot_generations;

     -- Also drop the exception-raising trigger from 20250923008000 if somehow still present.
     DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS prevent_user_positioned_modification_trigger ON shot_generations;

     COMMENT ON TABLE shot_generations IS
     'Timeline positions: user_positioned items can be freely updated by the owner.
     Automated standardization logic is permanently disabled via no-op function overrides
     in 20250925002000. The old BEFORE UPDATE protection triggers have been removed
     (20260218210000) as they also blocked legitimate user drag operations.';
     ```

     1. Client-side race: `syncFromDatabase` bypasses locks with `pendingCount: 0` — fixed with `writeInFlightRef`
     2. BEFORE UPDATE triggers silently reverting DB writes — migration `20260218210000` created to fix
   - **SKIP LOCKED migration patched wrong function**: Previous summary mentioned concern about function name mismatch. Verified: `20260218185000` creates trigger using `sync_shot_data_update_batch()`, and `20260218200000` patches exactly that function with SKIP LOCKED. Chain is correct.
   - **8s watchdog still fires despite SKIP LOCKED**: Unexplained. The BEFORE UPDATE triggers (silently reverting) may contribute if the AFTER trigger still blocks on `generations` rows. Dropping the BEFORE triggers may also improve this.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Identified root cause of "drag doesn't persist"**: BEFORE UPDATE triggers from Sept 2025 (`protect_user_positioned_timeline_frames_trigger`, `prevent_drag_position_overwrites_trigger`) check `OLD.metadata->>'drag_source' IS NOT NULL` and revert any frame change. After the first successful drag, ALL rows have `drag_source: 'drag'` in metadata, so ALL subsequent drags are silently reverted.
   - **Why mismatch check doesn't catch this**: `persistTimelineFrameBatch` verifies only that the same IDs are returned, not that the frame values match what was requested. The RETURNING clause returns all touched rows even if BEFORE triggers set frames back to old values.
   - **Client-side snap-back race (line 67)**: Despite `isLockedRef.current = true` and `isUpdatingRef.current = true` being set at line 52, `syncFromDatabase` somehow bypasses both at line 67. Root cause unclear (possibly stale closure, React concurrent mode subtlety, or second instance). Added `writeInFlightRef` as a hard atomic counter guard that cannot be bypassed.
   - **`writeInFlightRef` design**: Incremented AFTER `applyOptimistic` (before `try` block), decremented in `finally`. The `skipDatabase` early-return path does NOT increment (correct — no actual write). This ensures the counter exactly covers the DB write window.

**Analysis complete. Here's what we found and fixed:**

## What was wrong

The "instant revert" you see is a visual snap-back, not a data loss. Your positions **do** persist to the database — log lines 133 and 143 confirm the drag writes succeed and the DB reflects the new positions.

The visual snap happens because of a **cross-path race condition**:

1. You drag an image in the batch editor → `moveItemsToMidpoint` fires, starts a slow RPC (~12s due to PgBouncer connection pool saturation)
2. While the RPC is in-flight, an unrelated background refetch updates `shotGenerations` in React state
3. `useTimelinePositions.syncFromDatabase` sees the updated `shotGenerations` (still with old frames — the RPC hasn't finished yet) and snaps the timeline ruler positions back to the pre-drag values
4. The batch editor path (`useTimelineFrameUpdates`) never sets `isLockedRef` in `useTimelinePositions` — those are two separate hook instances with no shared state

## The fix (two files, minimal)

**`timelineWriteQueue.ts`** — exported a new function:
```typescript
export function isTimelineWriteActive(shotId: string): boolean {
  return (shotWriteDepth.get(shotId) ?? 0) > 0;
}
```

The write queue already tracks in-flight and queued writes per shot via `shotWriteDepth`. Both the timeline drag path and the batch editor path go through `runSerializedTimelineWrite`, so this counter covers all writes across both paths.

**`useTimelinePositions.ts`** — added a cross-path guard in `syncFromDatabase`:
```typescript
if (shotId && isTimelineWriteActive(shotId)) {
  // suppress stale sync during any in-flight write
  return;
}
```

This prevents `syncFromDatabase` from applying stale positions while any write (from either the drag path or batch editor path) is in-flight. Once the write completes and the subsequent refetch arrives with fresh data, the sync is allowed again with correct positions.

## The 6-12 second latency

This is PgBouncer connection pool saturation when workers are active. The actual DB query executes in 1-4s, but the client can wait 4-11s for a pooled connection. Nothing actionable client-side — the visual fix above makes this latency invisible to the user since positions no longer snap back while waiting.

Clean. Here's what these logs will now expose definitively:

**What each log tells you:**

**`[BatchEditorOrder] imagesWithBadges changed`** — fires every time the displayed order in the batch editor changes. Compare the timestamp with the drag-end:
- If it shows **old order** within milliseconds of the drag ending → the snap-back is here: the batch editor has no optimistic update, so it reverts to React Query's cached state the instant the DnD library releases its visual override. The RPC hasn't returned yet, so old order persists.
- If it shows **new order** immediately → batch editor is not the problem; look elsewhere.

**`[BatchEditorOrder] drag-end snapshot`** in `handleReorder` — fires at the exact moment the DnD gesture ends, before any async work. Shows `desiredOrder` (what the user dragged to) vs `currentOrder` (what React state shows right now). `orderMatchesCurrent: true` means the two already agree and there's no snap visible. `false` means there's a gap — the user sees the desired order during drag (DnD's visual), but React renders currentOrder once drag ends.

**`[TimelinePositionsPersist] positions state changed` with `caller`** — fires on every React state update to `positions`. If you see this with `caller: "syncFromDatabase"` during or after a drag, `syncFromDatabase` slipped through all our guards. If you never see it fire at all after a drag, the timeline ruler isn't snapping — the issue is in the batch editor.

**`[TimelinePositionsPersist] MOUNTED` / `UNMOUNTED`** — if UNMOUNTED fires during a drag, the component tree destroyed the hook and rebuilt it, wiping all optimistic state. No guard survives that.

**The expected diagnosis**: The `drag-end snapshot` log will show `orderMatchesCurrent: false` and the `[BatchEditorOrder]` log will immediately show old order after the drag — revealing that the batch editor path has no optimistic state at all. The fix would then be to apply an optimistic reorder to the displayed list while the RPC is in-flight (instead of waiting 12 seconds for the write to confirm).

The `applyOptimistic` call lives **inside** `runSerializedTimelineWrite`. The queue is blocked waiting for the midpoint RPC. When the user drags the timeline ruler, the visual update doesn't fire until the queue unblocks — which is 8–12 seconds later, or never if the hook unmounts first.

**Batch mode — stays visually but reverts on refresh:**
The DnD library holds the dragged item in place during the gesture. When drag ends, the library releases its visual state and React renders from `imagesWithBadges` — which still has old `timeline_frame` values because the RPC hasn't returned. The visual snap happens then. And if the user navigates away during the 8–12 second RPC, the fetch is cancelled, the DB transaction rolls back, nothing persists.

**Should they operate the same way?** Yes. Both should apply an optimistic update *immediately* when the drag ends, completely independent of the write queue. The queue serializes DB writes; it should never gate UI updates.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   - Fix timeline drag snap-back / position revert behavior that prevents drags from persisting visually
   - User reports three distinct behaviors: (1) timeline drag "moves back instantly", (2) batch mode items "kind of stay in place" but go back on refresh/return
   - User asked whether both modes should operate the same way and what complexity was being missed
   - User challenged: "Zoom out here and think about what Logs could actually get to the very, very bottom of this where there's no place to hide from it"
   - User provided three successive log files for diagnosis: `localhost-1771454521197`, `localhost-1771456811059`, `localhost-1771456967029`

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Two persistence paths**: Timeline drag path (`useTimelinePositions` / `usePositionManagement`) vs. Batch editor midpoint path (`useTimelineFrameUpdates`)
   - **`runSerializedTimelineWrite`**: Module-level per-shot write queue serializing all writes (both paths); `shotWriteDepth` tracks depth
   - **`isTimelineWriteActive(shotId)`**: New export — returns `true` if any write is active/queued for that shot
   - **`applyOptimistic`**: Immediate visual state update inside `useTimelinePositions`; currently gated by write queue in `usePositionManagement.ts`
   - **`syncFromDatabase`**: Auto-syncs positions from `shotGenerations` props; has guards: `isLockedRef`, `isUpdatingRef`, `writeInFlightRef > 0`, `isTimelineWriteActive`
   - **Cross-path guard bug**: `isTimelineWriteActive` guard blocked initial mount sync, leaving timeline empty when hook mounted during an in-flight batch editor RPC
   - **Mount/unmount lifecycle**: Timeline hook (`useTimelinePositions`) mounts when user navigates to timeline tab, unmounts when leaving — if a midpoint RPC is running, queued drags never execute
   - **Optimistic update architectural gap**: `applyOptimistic` is INSIDE `runSerializedTimelineWrite` in `usePositionManagement.ts` — when queue is blocked (8-12s RPC), NO visual update fires; DnD library releases, instant snap-back
   - **Batch editor has NO optimistic update**: DnD library provides transient visual during gesture; when gesture ends, React renders from `imagesWithBadges` (old order); if user navigates away mid-RPC, fetch cancelled, nothing persists
   - **PgBouncer connection pool**: 8-12 second RPC latency from client perspective (5-10s pool wait + 1-4s actual DB query)
   - **`setPositionsCallerRef`**: Ref tracking which code path last called `setPositions` — logged in `positions state changed` effect for attribution
   - **`batch_update_timeline_frames`**: SECURITY DEFINER set-based RPC; authenticated role has `statement_timeout=8s`

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **`isTimelineWriteActive` guard blocked initial mount sync**: The cross-path guard `if (shotId && isTimelineWriteActive(shotId))` worked correctly for ongoing syncs but also suppressed the INITIAL sync when the hook first mounts (positions.size = 0). This left the timeline blank whenever it mounted while a batch editor write was in-flight. Fix: added `&& positionsRef.current.size > 0` condition — only suppress if positions are already loaded (something to protect), always allow sync on initial mount.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Confirmed data DOES persist**: Log lines showed correct positions in liveTimeline fetch and `pending merge {verified: 11}` — confirming DB writes succeed. Issue is purely visual.
   - **Cross-path guard**: `isTimelineWriteActive` correctly prevents syncFromDatabase from overwriting positions during any write (batch editor or timeline). Fixed the initial-mount edge case.
   - **Root cause of timeline instant snap identified definitively**: `applyOptimistic` is inside `runSerializedTimelineWrite`. When queue is blocked by a slow midpoint RPC (8-12s), timeline drags queue up but their visual update never fires. Queue unblocks after 8-12s (or never if hook unmounts). DnD library releases, React renders from old state — instant snap.
   - **Root cause of batch editor revert on refresh**: No optimistic update for image order. DnD library provides transient visual during gesture only. If user navigates away during 8-12s RPC, fetch is cancelled, DB rolls back, nothing persists.
   - **Common root**: Both modes need optimistic updates applied IMMEDIATELY on drag-end, independent of write queue. Queue should serialize only DB writes, never gate UI updates.

Now I have a clear picture of what needs to be fixed. Let me implement it.

**Root cause confirmed:** In `usePositionManagement.ts`, `setFramePositions` wraps ALL of `positionSystem.updatePositions()` — including `applyOptimistic` — inside `runSerializedTimelineWrite`. When the queue is blocked by an 8-12s midpoint RPC, the visual update never fires. Fix: apply optimistic IMMEDIATELY before the queue, only queue the DB write.

The tricky part: `updatePositions` computes changes by diffing `newPositions` against `positionsRef.current`. If optimistic was pre-applied, `positionsRef.current` already reflects the new positions → diff = 0 → early return, DB write skipped. Solution: use `snapshotRef.current` (set by `applyOptimistic` before the state update) for change calculation when `skipOptimistic: true`.
